SYK — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
business-model
  • The two segments arrive at almost identical operating margins from opposite cost structures. Orthopaedics runs a 72.9% segment gross margin against 33.1% of sales in SG&A, because implants are consigned to hospital shelves and sold by a rep standing in the operating room. MedSurg and Neurotechnology runs a 62.6% segment gross margin against 25.1% SG&A, because a camera platform or a defibrillator is sold once to a capital committee
  • Stryker therefore owns the implant inventory sitting in every hospital's consignment cupboard until a surgeon opens the box, which is why consolidated inventory was $5,310m at YE2025, 21.1% of sales, 67% of it in finished form, and why days inventory outstanding has never dropped below 197 in the decade.
  • Mako plus enabling technologies plus bone cement is therefore about 6% of the restated segment, and Mako-branded revenue alone is less than that. The robot pulls through the 40% of segment revenue that is knees and hips; it is not itself a revenue line of consequence.
  • MedSurg was the only source of positive price in the group and it is gone: the contribution ran +1.7% (FY2023), +1.7% (FY2024), +0.7% (FY2025), +0.3% (H1 2026) and +0.1% (Q2 2026), and the Q2 2026 segment margin bridge has no sales-pricing line at all.
  • The $921m of transferred revenue therefore came with $435m of operating income, a 47.2% segment operating margin, on a 64.4% gross margin and 8.6% of revenue in SG&A.
  • The capital-equipment core of MedSurg — Instruments, Endoscopy and Medical, 72% of the segment — runs at roughly a 57% segment gross margin, some 550bps below the segment average and about 1,600bps below Orthopaedics
  • It shows a console at $48,337 on an 8–10 year replacement cycle, attached hardware adding 16.8% at the point of sale, prepaid service at 4.1% of console price a year, a data subscription on top, and a trade-in credit that makes the incumbent's replacement cheaper than a competitor's new install.
  • Hospital capital spending is funded out of hospital operating cash flow, so the exposure is the US hospital operating margin with a one-to-two-year lag, plus healthcare construction put-in-place for the OR-infrastructure revenue Endoscopy named as its top Q2 2026 US growth driver.
  • After removing Inari, Vascular contributed roughly 4% of MedSurg's FY2023–25 organic growth on 13% of its revenue. It is an option on a trial readout, not a demonstrated driver.
competitive-advantage
  • Stryker's Orthopaedics segment earns returns well above its cost of capital and the gap over every listed competitor is widening on most measures. The advantage is not the kind that lets the business set its own price. It is a distribution-and-workflow position that converts into volume and share, it has to be paid for with continuous reinvestment in the robot and with acquisitions, and it depends on four rivals continuing to execute worse.
  • On the segment measure Stryker disclosed for FY2025, Orthopaedics earned a 29.8% operating margin against 14.9% for Smith+Nephew's directly comparable Orthopaedics segment and roughly 24.6% for Zimmer Biomet's whole company on a like-for-like adjusted basis
  • Zimmer Biomet's global knee revenue of $3,322m and hip revenue of $2,094m both exceed Stryker's $2,656m and $1,865m, so Stryker is second by revenue in both categories among the four majors even after a decade of Mako.
  • in the most recent clean quarter the pull-through claim failed a head-to-head test: Stryker's hips grew 2.8% while Johnson & Johnson's hips, sold without any comparable robot, grew 4.9%
  • But the lock has a ceiling and excludes nobody: the hospital keeps buying implants from three other vendors for its non-Mako surgeons, and only 30-40% of Mako sites have more than one system.
  • Mako has arrested price erosion rather than reversed it. Orthopaedics price declined about 1.95% a year on average over 2017-2022 and about 0.4% a year over 2023-2025, with 0.0% in both quarters of 2026.
  • Intuitive Surgical is the closest structural match: a capital platform placed into the operating room that annuitizes a per-procedure revenue stream and locks the surgeon's technique to the platform. It holds because Mako, like da Vinci, runs only Stryker's own implants, so the installed base is a closed channel. It breaks because da Vinci faces no equivalently funded alternative in soft-tissue surgery while Mako faces ROSA, CORI, VELYS and Excelsius, and because manual arthroplasty remains a clinically accepted, cheaper fallback.
  • The MedSurg advantage is real, category by category rather than company-wide, and it earns excess returns today. It is not structural in the sense that would make it safe from a well-funded attacker, and two of the three erosion markers are already visible in the disclosed numbers.
  • the segment compounded revenue at 13.4% a year from $8,345m (2020) to $15,647m (2025) while segment operating margin went from 28.9% to 29.9% and segment gross margin rose from 59.9% (2023) to 62.6% (2025)
  • Stryker's consolidated ROIC at a normalized 21% tax was 11.4% in 2019 and 11.9% in 2025 — flat over six years while revenue grew 69%, against Boston Scientific moving 9.3% to 12.5% and Medtronic 7.6% to 12.2%.
  • Under 10% of group sales are recognised as services over time, and Stryker discloses no recurring-revenue percentage, renewal rate, attach rate, backlog dollar or software-subscription figure anywhere.
  • Excluding goodwill and intangibles, ROIC stayed in a 30-40% band throughout and was 37.6% in 2025 against 38.9% in 2019. The operating businesses earn very high returns on tangible capital, and the acquired capital dilutes the consolidated figure.
competitive-landscape
  • Three of the four majors independently describe the same structure. Stryker's 10-K names Zimmer, Johnson & Johnson MedTech and Smith & Nephew; Zimmer Biomet names the identical three in reverse
  • Stryker gained 191bps of the four-player hip-and-knee pool while Zimmer Biomet lost 95bps, J&J MedTech 59bps and Smith+Nephew 38bps, and Stryker passed J&J into the second hip position in 2024.
  • Extremities is the one place a competitor is credibly taking share from Stryker.
  • Sports medicine is the one sub-market inside Endoscopy with a filing-sourced share table, and it is where Stryker is not the leader. Smith+Nephew sizes it at $7.0bn in 2025 growing 6% and gives Arthrex 35%, Smith+Nephew 25%, Stryker 13%, DePuy Synthes 9% and others 18%
  • Against the only listed comparator that discloses the line, Stryker Medical at $4,204m is 2.2x Baxter's Care and Connectivity Solutions at $1,911m, and grew 13.4% in Q2 2026 against Baxter's 5%.
  • Penumbra's thrombectomy line grew 12.5% to $259.0m in calendar Q2 2026 and 18.3% on a two-year CAGR, while Stryker Vascular declined 0.7% reported and 6.7% in the United States in the same quarter.
  • What it means is that the device revenue actually spent in the two core indications today is $932m, and the remaining roughly $5bn is patients receiving anticoagulation, compression or systemic thrombolytics valued as if each converted to a device procedure at current prices.
critical-points
  • Weighting by prior-year half revenue, the 8.8% full-year midpoint requires about 11.5% in H2, the largest half-over-half acceleration Stryker has ever guided to
  • Roughly $2.0bn a year of Orthopaedic Instruments moved from MedSurg into a new Ortho Tech line inside Orthopaedics; Neuro Cranial and spine Enabling Technologies moved into Instruments; $518m of goodwill was reallocated on a relative-fair-value basis, creating a new reporting unit.
expectations
  • Consensus's 8.5% revenue CAGR for 2025-2028 sits at roughly the 90th percentile of the realised medtech distribution above $20bn of revenue, where the median outcome was 1.3%.
financial-history
  • Gross margin declined 350bps from 66.3% (2016) to 62.8% (2022) and recovered 120bps to 64.0% (2025) without regaining the pre-2020 level, and management says it will not: segment mix runs the wrong way because the lower-gross-margin segment grows faster.
  • Spinal Implants revenue ran $713m (2023), $707m (2024), $185m (2025) and $3m in Q2 2026 after the April 2025 divestiture, and is the whole of the apparent Orthopaedics slowdown: the segment grew 10.9% ex-spine in 2025 against 4.3% as reported.
forward-model
  • The base case sits at roughly the 75th-80th percentile of the appropriate reference class on growth and around the 45th percentile on margin expansion.
management-claims
  • the disclosed 9-point shift out of hips and knees produces roughly 81bps of weighted-average uplift at a generous 9-point growth-rate gap between the exiting and receiving categories, not the 200bps claimed
  • At the MedSurg segment level, operating margin was 28.9% in FY2020 and 29.9% in FY2025 on 88% more revenue, and the incremental operating margin across that whole span was 30.9%, within a point of the 29.9% average margin.
  • Management points to two consecutive years of at least 100bps of adjusted operating margin expansion, 200bps promised and delivered over three years, and a further minimum 150bps through 2028.
  • Reported segment margin rose 130bps to 29.8%. Ex-Spinal Implants, the ongoing business's margin was -59bps if the divested unit earned 0% and -123bps if it earned -10%
  • Nine weeks later management guided FY2026 adjusted EPS to $14.90-$15.10 against FY2025's $13.63, a range of +9.3% to +10.8%; the Q2 2026 narrowing moved the floor to +9.7%, still short.
secular-trends
  • Average organic growth over 2016-2019 was 7.4% and over 2023-2025 was 10.7%, an acceleration of 329bps. Average price moved from (1.20)% to +0.70%, worth 190bps or 58% of that acceleration. Average unit volume moved from 8.58% to 9.97%, worth 139bps or 42%.
  • Knees plus Hips, the whole Mako-pulled implant business, generated $353m, 15.3% of the $2,300m of ongoing dollars added. Trauma and Extremities alone generated more, and its drivers are the Wright Medical shoulder portfolio and the Pangea plating launch, not Mako.
  • Knee revenue compounded at 6.6% over 2015-2019 and 6.5% over 2019-2025, across the period in which the installed base passed 3,000 systems in 45-plus countries, US knee utilization reached over two-thirds and cumulative procedures passed two million.
  • By Q2 2026 that premium had narrowed sharply in the flagship lines: US Knees +6.2% against a cohort running 2-5%, and US Hips +4.9% against Smith+Nephew's +5.8% and J&J's +3.5%, so Stryker's hips now grow in line with or below the peer group.
  • Recomputed from a pre-COVID base, compounding the disclosed organic rates from 2019 gives 59.4% cumulative growth over six years, an 8.1% organic CAGR. Against the 6% weighted-average served-market growth rate that is roughly 205bps of outgrowth
  • Organic growth was 8.1% in 2019, 10.3% in 2025 and guided at 8.3-9.3% for 2026; unit volume was 9.0% in 2019 and about 8.0-9.0% implied for 2026.
technology
  • Stryker is not a frontier-technology company. It buys categories and then out-iterates the incumbents inside them, and the thing that is hard to replicate is the combination of a twenty-year installed base of robots that anchors implant choice, an implant-plus-robot ecosystem that is deliberately closed, a sales organisation specialised down to 22 business units, and a generational launch cadence that customers budget around.
  • The surgeon still cuts; the robot prevents the cut from leaving the plan. What it replaces is a mechanical cutting jig, and in the more advanced comparison, computer-assisted navigation. What it sells is a repeatable, teachable procedure that de-risks the case for a lower-volume surgeon and for an ambulatory centre.
  • The installed base went from roughly 650 systems at YE2018 to about 1,500 at YE2021 and over 3,000 at YE2025 in more than 45 countries, and cumulative procedures passed two million across Total Knee, Total Hip and Partial Knee
  • The clinical evidence does not support the claim that Mako produces better patient outcomes than conventional or computer-assisted knee surgery.
  • The substitution threat is imageless handheld robotics at a lower capital step-up, and Stryker has already conceded the segment.
  • Zimmer Biomet has stated a roadmap for a semi-autonomous Monogram mBôs platform in early 2027 followed by a fully autonomous platform at the end of 2027 or early 2028
  • every major competitor now offers 3D-printed cementless options, so the durable asset is the ten-year registry data, which erodes as rivals' datasets mature
  • This is the one franchise where technology demonstrably supports price, and it is the one that moved into Orthopaedics in the Q1 2026 recast.
  • What differentiates Stryker other than technology may matter more: 22 business units each with their own general manager, sales force, marketing, R&D and business-development lead; a commission structure set on pricing and discount level so reps are paid to defend price; 460-plus ASC partnerships averaging seven business units per deal; and roughly 60 acquisitions in ten years plugged into specialised sales forces by integration teams that move from deal to deal.
value-chain
  • Stryker is not the highest-margin layer in its own chain: outsourced sterilization, a mandatory and FDA-validated step for essentially every implant and sterile disposable Stryker ships and a dependency Stryker names by function in its own risk factors, earns 46-55% operating or segment-income margins against Stryker's 19.5% consolidated GAAP operating margin.
  • HCA cut supplies from 16.5% of revenue in 2018-2019 to 15.0% in 2025 and 14.3% in Q2 2026 — 220bps of the customer's revenue line reclaimed from the supply chain over seven years, a larger transfer than the 100-150bps of price Stryker recovered.
  • Against the $9,393 Medicare ambulatory payment, the $5,336 mean implant plus the robot consume $7,193, or 77% of the payment, before staff, facility or anaesthesia.
  • The documented economic benefit of robotic knee replacement accrues to the payor, not the buyer of the robot.

The two segments arrive at almost identical operating margins from opposite cost structures. Orthopaedics runs a 72.9% segment gross margin against 33.1% of sales in SG&A, because implants are consigned to hospital shelves and sold by a rep standing in the operating room. MedSurg and Neurotechnology runs a 62.6% segment gross margin against 25.1% SG&A, because a camera platform or a defibrillator is sold once to a capital committee

Stryker's Orthopaedics segment earns returns well above its cost of capital and the gap over every listed competitor is widening on most measures. The advantage is not the kind that lets the business set its own price. It is a distribution-and-workflow position that converts into volume and share, it has to be paid for with continuous reinvestment in the robot and with acquisitions, and it depends on four rivals continuing to execute worse.

Intuitive Surgical is the closest structural match: a capital platform placed into the operating room that annuitizes a per-procedure revenue stream and locks the surgeon's technique to the platform. It holds because Mako, like da Vinci, runs only Stryker's own implants, so the installed base is a closed channel. It breaks because da Vinci faces no equivalently funded alternative in soft-tissue surgery while Mako faces ROSA, CORI, VELYS and Excelsius, and because manual arthroplasty remains a clinically accepted, cheaper fallback.

The da Vinci surgical system generally sells for between $0.7 million and $3.1 million (generally inclusive of one year of service), depending on the model, configuration, and geography, and represents a significant capital equipment investment for our customers when purchased. Stryker's consolidated ROIC at a normalized 21% tax was 11.4% in 2019 and 11.9% in 2025 — flat over six years while revenue grew 69%, against Boston Scientific moving 9.3% to 12.5% and Medtronic 7.6% to 12.2%.

The awards include three metrics: relative total shareholder return (rTSR), revenue growth, and return on invested capital (ROIC). rTSR is considered a market condition metric, and the expense is determined at the grant date and will not be adjusted even if the market condition is not met. Revenue growth and ROIC are considered performance metrics, and the expense is recorded over the performance period, which will be reassessed each reporting period based on the probability of achieving the various performance conditions.

In the first quarter 2026 we announced a change in our organizational structure. Our new Ortho Tech business combines the orthopaedic instruments portfolio (Orthopaedic Instruments) from Instruments with Other Orthopaedics. In addition, Neuro Cranial and the spine enabling technologies portfolio (Enabling Technologies) from Other Orthopaedics were combined with the remaining Instruments business to align with our internal reporting structure. Ortho Tech includes sales related to Orthopaedic Instruments of $523 and $501 and Other Orthopaedics of $194 and $148 for the three months 2026 and 2025. For the six months 2026 and 2025 Ortho Tech includes sales related to Orthopaedic Instruments of $1,012 and $985 and Other Orthopaedics of $351 and $281. Instruments includes sales related to Neuro Cranial of $681 and $616 and Enabling Technologies of $28 and $34 for the three months 2026 and 2025. For the six months 2026 and 2025 Instruments includes sales related to Neuro Cranial of $1,287 and $1,179 and Enabling Technologies of $54 and $63. We have reflected these changes in all historical periods presented.
— Stryker Corporation Form 10-Q, Q2 2026, 2026-07-31

As of the beginning of fiscal year 2024, the Company realigned the operating segment structure as a result of how the Chief Operating Decision Maker assesses business performance. We continue to have four reportable segments: Cardiovascular Portfolio, Neuroscience Portfolio, Medical Surgical Portfolio, and Diabetes Operating Unit. The Medical Surgical Portfolio now consists of two operating segments which have been aggregated based upon similar economic and operating characteristics. Prior period segment operating profit has been recast to reflect the new reporting structure, which primarily includes allocating certain prior Medical Surgical businesses to the Other line.
— Medtronic plc Form 10-Q, fiscal Q1 2024 (period ended 2023-07-28), 2023-08-31
filingqualifies

On March 9, 2026, MiniMed completed an initial public offering (the IPO). As a result, during the fourth quarter of fiscal year 2026, the Diabetes Operating Unit was no longer considered a reportable segment.

The entire $700 million is expected to be divested to them. In total, 22 articles evaluating 3,738 TKAs were included: 1,835 MTKAs and 1,903 RTKAs. The evidence level for most studies were IIa, due to few high-level studies. ... In the first quarter 2026 we announced a change in our organizational structure. Our new Ortho Tech business combines the orthopaedic instruments portfolio from our Instruments business with the Mako and enabling technologies portfolio from our Other Orthopaedics business. By bringing Mako, power tools, cutting accessories, enabling technologies and the teams behind these products together under one business, we are simplifying the customer experience and striving to increase our speed to market through focused innovation. Following this reorganization we continue to have two business segments, (i) MedSurg and Neurotechnology and (ii) Orthopaedics, each of which comprise a reportable segment. All historical segment financial information has been recast to conform to this new reporting structure in our financial statements and accompanying notes.
— Stryker Corporation Q2 2026 Form 10-Q, 2026-06-30

What differentiates Stryker other than technology may matter more: 22 business units each with their own general manager, sales force, marketing, R&D and business-development lead; a commission structure set on pricing and discount level so reps are paid to defend price; 460-plus ASC partnerships averaging seven business units per deal; and roughly 60 acquisitions in ten years plugged into specialised sales forces by integration teams that move from deal to deal.
